WILLIAM R. HOLLAND
Superintendent of Schools
BURTON E. GOODRICH, JR.
Administrative Assistant
It is a privilege to submit my Third Annual Report as Superintendent of Lunenburg Public Schools. This is
the 103rd such report preserved in the Town records and covers the year ending December 31, 1972.
LOOKING BACK ON 1972 - THE SCHOOL YEAR IN CAPSULE
Lunenburg Schools experienced a relatively smooth year in 1972. Negotiations with the teachers' associ-
ation and non-teaching groups proceeded without undue conflict and although school costs continued to climb a
School Budget of $2,069,069. passed without opposition from the voters at the Annual Town Meeting in March
Mrs. Marjorie Stillman was returned by the voters to the School Committee after an absence of six years and Mr.
Roger Raboin was also elected to a 3-year term. These members replaced Mrs. Jean Larkin and Mr. Lou Forgues
both of whom completed full 3-year terms and did not choose to run for re-election.
School enrollment continued to remain quite stable with our 2226 students on October 1st representing an
increase of 13 students over 1971 figures. Anticipating a predicted decline in elementary enrollment last Spring,
one teacher was transferred to the Middle School to fill a vacancy thereby resulting in a cutback of one 4th grade
teacher. Surprisingly, however, the decline in enrollment did not materialize and instead we added more students
in our middle grades--the grades where we are currently experiencing an enrollment bulge.
The school year was highlighted last Spring by a well-attended Open Forum concerning smoking in the
High School. Parents and students discussed the pro's and con's of establishing an outside smoking lounge at Lunen-
burg High School. Both parents, the Administration, and the School Committee agreed that smoking should not be
condoned in the High School under any circumstances since it is a health problem and should be discouraged by
school authorities. The end result of the open forum and the collective study of the smoking problem was:
(1) a more realistic discipline policy was established for students found smoking in lavatories
(2) greater parent involvement and responsibility were called for on individual smoking
problems, and
(3) a mass clean-up campaign of the High School lavatories and a general appeal to
the student body were initiated
-86-
===PAGE 89===
In retrospect, although the smoking problem has
not disappeared, the condition of the lavatories has im-
proved and it can be safe to state that relatively speak-
ing, Lunenburg High does not have the severe smoking
problem other high schools do.
In other developments, the School Committee
approved a pilot winter intramural/recreational pro -
gram for students in grades 8 through 12 and established
a core program in grades 8 and 9 for those students
who are underachievers and need more individual help
and instruction in reading and math skills.
Responding to direction from the State Depart -
ment of Education, a fifteen (15) member Citizens Ad-
visory Committee was appointed and assisted the admin-
istration in drafting educational goals for the Lunen-
burg Schools. Also, a five (5) member Student
Advisory Committee was elected and has met several
times with the School Committee for purposes of dis-
cussing issues of concern to students. These meetings
As runner fleeing by, another school year
also serve as an opportunity for the School Committee
passes
to explain its function, its problems, and its plans with
representative High School students.
The Work/Study Program continued to grow with enrollment totaling over thirty (30) students. The first
objective evaluation of the program revealed a statistically significant improvement in the attendance record of
work/study students. Discussions to consider possible areas of collaboration also began with Monty Tech represent-
atives and at this point the first out-reach course, "Concepts in Data-Processing" is tentatively planned for this
Winter. This undertaking would be the first step in using the superb facilities at Monty Tech after school or during
the summer vacation for short-term occupational skills courses for Lunenburg High undergraduates who otherwise
would not get such an opportunity. In a similar development, the High School Industrial Arts curriculum was reor-
ganized into several occupational clusters with various courses offered in areas of technical drawing, general metals,
general woods, and power mechanics. Currently, we
are awaiting approved federal funds for a graphic
communications technology project--a program that
would benefit Business Education students as well as
students who elect other occupational education
courses. Our long-range plan is to create an OCCU -
pational education model that would serve approxi -
mately 35% of our student body by teaching basic job.
-entry skills and helping students make realistic
occupational choices upon graduation. These occu-
pational courses will serve as but one component ina
planned K-12 career education program for Lunen-
burg Schools. For example, the Middle School has
also been actively planning a career education pro -
gram this year which includes career days, visits to
local places of employment, role playing of job
interviews, information on careers and other explor-
atory experiences in career education for Middle
Schoolers. Future plans call for the continuation of
the career education design in the elementary grades.

In our excitement to exclaim the new and the different, let US not forget the "tried and true"
staff who have given so much over the years to make our schools what they are today.

In summary, 1972 was a year where much
happened in Lunenburg Schools without much fan -
fare. For example, Lunenburg was selected by the
Massachusetts Council of Arts and Humanities as one
of the three school systems in the State to host
an Artist-in-Residence program. Our resident artist,
Mr. John Caldwell, who works primarily with plas -
tics and with polarized light effects, has materially
contributed to our school system and added new di -
mensions to our Art program. Mini-courses contined
to enjoy popularity with Middle School students;
parent coffee/discussion hours and an early identifi-
cation of learning difficulties program for pre kin-
dergarten youngsters were initiated by the elemen -
tary staff; and suddenly, mainly due to the efforts
and free labor of our Supervisor of Buildings and
Grounds, Mr. Lemieux, our long-awaited school
garage was erected. The Elementary and Middle
School staffs started to take a critical look at their
math, science, and social studies programs;
our Basketball and Spring Track teams won champion-
John Caldwell, Resident Artist, adds cultural
ships; and several first grade teachers developed a
dimension to Lunenburg Schools.
team-teaching plan that utilizes several techniques
and practices found in "open classrooms".
In spite of these many accomplishments, however, 1972 was a year where the Lunenburg School System
took a good, close look at its goals and its future direction.
FIVE YEAR GOALS FOR LUNENBURG SCHOOLS
Since 1970, school administrators in cooperation with their faculties, have been asked to develop yearly
Master plans. These plans simply represent a respective school's goals for the year or stated another way, their
targets for improvement.
These plans are incorporated into an annual
master plan for the school system prepared by the
Superintendent and presented to the School Committee
and the Community for their approval, and hopefully
financial endorsement. Yearly master plans, however,
must point to some long-range direction for Lunen -
burg Schools over a period of years. Parents, students,
and teachers may not totally agree with the stated
long-range goals and may want to change or modify
them; however, at least some planning has been done
(planning which should be scrutinized), some points
of departure and hopeful arrival have been articulated,
and a means for future evaluation has been provided.
With these thoughts in mind, the following list repre-
sents 5-year Goals for Lunenburg Schools accompanied
by the more specific 18-month Master Plan Goals.
These goals were drafted this past year and presented
to the Lunenburg School Committee:
Karen Plunkett, Football Cheerleader, enjoys Lion's
Club Barbeque served up by Edwin Cassano and Ellen
Brown at annual homecoming.
-89-
===PAGE 92===
1. GOAL: Help all students before graduating from high school establish alternative career goals. Help
all students who terminate education in Grade 12 locate employment within 3-6 months after graduation. MASTER
PLAN TARGETS: (a) More clearly develop our emerging career education curriculum particularly in grades 5-12--
programs should include awareness and exploratory activities in the Elementary and Middle Schools and career de -
velopment experiences for all High School students (tutoring, cooperative work programs, internships, volunteer
work, independent study). Such programs call for changes in the current High School Schedule; (b) Immediately
begin to develop occupational courses for Lunenburg High students--this means updating and expanding current
Industrial Arts area and implementing out-reach programs with Monty Tech; (c)Take active measures to fill our stu-
dent quota at Monty Tech.
2. GOAL: Develop increased participation and understanding between the school and the home concern -
ing the educational process and new trends in education. MASTER PLAN TARGETS: (a) Form a Citizens Advisory
Council--have it serve as a reaction/recommendation group to the Superintendent and the School Committee. This
group should meet several times during the school year; (b) Develop the High School Student Advisory Committee
(five High School students elected by the student body) into a vital, contributing advisory group; (c) Encourage
and design activities that create greater "openness" in our schools and provide for parent in-put and support and
increased parent knowledge about the direction in which Lunenburg Schools are going (e. g. coffee hours, open
houses, visitation days, increased newspaper coverage, open forums, High School department nights, parent volun-
teers, advisory groups, career days, mini-courses, parent surveys, etc.).
3. GOAL: Identify school facilities that need updating, expansion and modernizing, establish plans of
action and begin feasible and attainable remodeling programs. Begin to look at school building needs for the
1980's and 1990's. MASTER PLAN TARGETS: (a) Implement the remaining 3rd year recommendations in the
5-year plan to update the Elementary Annex; (b) Have High School department heads in Industrial Arts, Science,
Physical Education, Art and Library/Media develop specifications for renovation and expansion of their respective
learning areas--such planning will be presented to the School Committee in the annual Departmental reports;(c)Be-
gin gradual replacement of old elementary classroom furniture (our surplus furniture supply is depleted); (d)Develop
a 5-year Preventative Maintenance Program for Lunenburg Schools.
4. GOAL: Have students by the end of 7th grade reach a level of mathematical achievement that empha-
sizes individual learning rates and development rather than remediation. Develop a more diagnostic program where
every year a student's skills mastery will be compared with established norms for his age level. MASTER PLAN
TARGETS: (a) Study new programs that allow for more individualized and diagnostic approaches to math in-
struction. Phase in such an improved math program over the next two years in the elementary grades.
5. GOAL: Implement a continuing teacher training and professional growth program for Lunenburg teach-
ers and administrators. MASTER PLAN TARGETS: (a) In collaboration with the Merrimack Education Center and
neighboring colleges and universities, continue to provide needed in-service courses for Lunenburg teachers. These
course experiences can continue to be offered here in Lunenburg after school; (b) Continue to refine emergency
structure for K-12 Curriculum coordination and curriculum leadership--earmark funds for use for teacher workshops
during released time, for consultant help, for curriculum writing, for curriculum research and particularly for cur-
riculum evaluation.
6. GOAL: Have students achieve a standard established for the communication skills (listening, speaking,
reading, writing) by the end of grade seven (similar to the plan outlined for math skills). MASTER PLAN TARGETS:
(a) Develop a more performance based curriculum in grammar, composition, and reading, particularly in the com -
munication skills; (b) Study the pupil progress reporting system and scheduling and grouping practices in the ele-
mentary school, particularly as they relate to reading and Language Arts.
7. GOAL: More actively guide students to patterns of learning which emphasize self-discovery and stu-
dent involvement rather than retention of information given by textbooks and teachers. MASTER PLAN TARGETS:
(a) Introduce lab-oriented science materials and activities in the Middle School. (Begin to pilot the same in the
elementary school although Math is a greater priority in 1973-74); (b) Evaluate the pilot Data Bank Social Studies
program used by Mr. Bishop in Grade 6 and expland this program if the results so warrant.
8. GOAL: Expose all students to a Comprehensive Health Education program which includes concepts and
values in areas of physical health, fitness and body care, nutrition, emotional health, well-being and personality
-90-
===PAGE 93===
development, safety, affects of alcohol, drugs and tobacco, and sex education. MASTER PLAN TARGETS
(a) Purchase necessary materials and provide for necessary teacher training and released-time in order to finish
construction of K-7 Health Curriculum. Have citizen's advisory council help the Health Committee complete the
implementation of the K-12 Health Curriculum model; (b) Consider the future need for a combined K-12 Physical
Education specialist and Health teacher.
9. GOAL: Help all students develop patterns for the use of leisure time and life-long learning and enjoy-
ment. MASTER PLAN TARGETS: (a) Evaluate High School Winter Intramural program and if successful provide for
expansion of intramural program in all grades; (b) Continue to build on the success of the Middle School mini-
courses and the High School activities format. Consider bringing in outside resource people and/or use current staff
to conduct mini-workshops (e. g. twice a week for 3 - 4 weeks in yoga, guitar, seminars on "hot" political/social
issues, etc.) as alternatives to high school study periods.
10. GOAL: Insure that all students whilein Lun-
enburg Schools experience or are exposed to activities
in the fine and performing arts, provide for more in -
depth experience in these areas for those students with
greater interest and/or talents. MASTER PLAN TAR
GETS: (a) Consider the introduction of a Theatre Arts
elective (1/2 year course that goes back to back with
Cinema). Strive for collaboration between English,
Music, and Art departments in this venture; (b) Con-
sider the introduction of more outside performing arts
programs for Lunenburg students particularly ele -
mentary students.
11. GOAL: More definitely provide success and
special training to the educationally, culturally, emo-
tionally and/or physically handicapped child. Speci-
fically, have the "Special Education" staff more
clearly articulate their role and services to the teach-
ing staff and to the community. Evaluate these services
in light of new State regulations and the new responsi-
bilities that must now be assumed by local school
systems. MASTER PLAN TARGETS: Disseminate an
information brochure to teachers and parents on pupil
Lisa Roux, Grade 4F, hoping to teach
personnel services available in Lunenburg schools; (b)
the world to sing.
Encourage greater activity by the Guidance staff --
activity that involves parents in dialogue about them-
selves, their children, the role of the school, and de-
cisions that must be made about the present and the
future; (c) Encourage the High School Guidance staff
to promote programs of student interest and student
need.
12. GOAL: Have teachers and Lunenburg Schools continue to move toward the creation of learning cli-
mates for children which are highly supportive and success-oriented in nature. In addition to academic achieve -
ment, the emotional development and well-being of students should also be a major component of such learning
climates. MASTER PLAN TARGETS: (a) Evaluate current introduction of "open classroom" and team - teaching
techniques in the 1st grade. If innovations look promising encourage the incorporation of these techniques in other
elementary classrooms; (b) As follow-up to current in-service training, devise teacher evaluation procedures and
techniques which help teachers acquire new human relations skills in their teaching; (c) Consciously recruit new
teachers to Lunenburg who possess the flexibility, background and personality that relates directly to the learning
climate just described; (d) Provide opportunities where appropriate for High School students, particularly Seniors,
to more actively accept individual responsibility and allow those who are capable more freedom of choice about
their own education.
-91-
===PAGE 94===
SCHOOL COMMITTEE ACTIVITIES
As of this writing, a busy Lunenburg School Committee finds itself involved in contract negotiations with
teachers, custodians, and cafeteria workers. Although a professional negotiator represents the Committee in con-
tract discussions with the teachers' association, all committee members sit as a body in all negotiation sessions. The
Committee does all its own negotiating, however, with the AFL-CIO Cafeteria and Custodial Union representatives.
At this point, the chances for contract settlements with all groups prior to Town Meeting are rather slim.
In addition to the time-consuming business of negotiations, the School Committee has been busy working
on its 18-month (January 1, 1973 - June 30, 1974) School Budget. 18-Month budgets are being developed in
municipalities throughout the State this year in order
to provide for a transfer from a calendar budget year
to a July to June fiscal budget year.
Concerned about possible reduced State re-
imbursements and the effect of the 18-month School
Budget on the tax rate, some budget cut-backs have
been made by the School Committee this year in the
expense and capital outlay accounts. These reduc -
tions, however, are found in less than 10% the total
operating budget since over 90% of the school budget
includes salaries and fixed costs such as transporta -
tion, heat, and utilities.
To conclude, being a School Committee
member in Lunenburg is long, hard and many times
complex work. It is estimated that a School Com-
mittee member spends approximately 320 hours per
year (or 40 work days) on School Committee business
with most of this time being spent in late evening
meetings. Considering the personal and professional
obligations these members have on the outside, Lun-
enburg owes its School Committee adebt of gratitude
for their dedication and sheer perseverance.
Mrs. Virginia Welch, Superintendent's Secretary
and Business Supervisor, puts the finishing touches
on the School Section of the 1972 Town Report.
CONCLUSIONS
In conclusion to this my third annual report as Superintendent of Schools, I would personally like to thank
principals, teachers, students, school committee members and members of the non-teaching staff for their support
and dedicated service during the past year. Mr. Goodrich, my Assistant; Mrs. Welch, my Personal Secretary and
Business Supervisor; and Mr. Lemieux, our Supervisor/Foreman of Buildings and Grounds, deserve special thanks.
These three people are invaluable to me and truly do an outstanding job in anything they undertake,
We in Lunenburg should be proud of our schools. Our problems are really small when compared to prob -
lems in other school districts. Your interest and support are responsible for this current condition of excellence --
only your continued interest and support will keep it that way.
DR. WILLIAM R. HOLLAND
Superintendent of Schools

===PAGE 95===
Retirements
ANATOLE LIMOGES
1964 - 1972
"Nat" became a member of the custodial staff
on March 31, 1964, working as a roving custodian and
groundsman. He was an able painter and did a great
deal of painting, carpentry, and in-side maintenance
work during the winter months. In the spring and sum -
mer-time, Nat could always be found wearing his straw
hat sitting on his lawnmower keeping the grounds main-
tained in superb shape.
In addition to his varied duties, Nat was also
the Brooks House custodian.
Mr. Limoges retired on May 4, 1972. A lunch-
eon to honor his retirement was held in the Elementary
School. The large turnout at this affair was a true in-
dication of the many friends he had made in his employ-
ment at Lunenburg.
Nat's work and high standard of performance
were appreciated and respected by everyone in Lunen -
burg Schools. We wish him a happy retirement.
MISS MARGARET M. SHEEHAN
1942 - 1972
"Peg" Sheehan's retirement in June of 1972, completed
thirty years of teaching in the Lunenburg School System. In
1942, she was employed to teach grades one, two and three in
the West School. She continued to teach these grades until
1950 when she took a grade one and two combination. After
being assigned a single first grade, Peg was transferred to the
now Thomas C. Passios School in 1953, where she continued
as a first grade teacher until her retirement.
During her thirty years of tenure, Peg has always main-
tained a high level of teaching competence, striving to teach
children self-responsibility. She showed great respect for
those with whom she worked and manifested genuine pride in
the teaching profession.
All of those who have known her and her professional
dedication, wish her many happy years of retirement.
-93-
===PAGE 96===
RETIREMENTS (Continued)
MRS. GRACE L. TAYLOR
1957 - 1972
Mrs. Taylor has taught twenty-two years with fifteen
of them being in Lunenburg. Mrs. Taylor graduated from
Fitchburg Teachers' College in 1928 and then took a teach -
ing assignment on Long Island where she taught grades six,
seven, and eight in one room. In 1929, an inspector arrived
to view her 55 children from wall-to-wall. He asked her if
they were having an assembly. She retorted that these child -
ren were hers all day long and that she had trouble telling
where one grade began and another ended. Mrs. Taylor had
had enough of Long Island. The following year--her third
year of teaching--she taught 5th and 6th grade math in a
suburb of Philadelphia where teaching conditions were improved.
Upon moving to Fitchburg, Mass. in 1957, Mrs. Taylor
was urged by a friend late in that summer to go back to teach-
ing. Fortunately for Lunenburg, she was hired as a 6th grade
teacher by Supt. Billings, two days before school opened that
September.
During her 15 years in Lunenburg, Mrs. Taylor moved from the Thomas C. Passios Elementary School to the Jr.
High School and back again. Then, after the Middle School was completed, she moved to new quarters once more.
Mrs. Taylor was a dedicated teacher in every sense of the word. Students and teachers alike developed a
keen sense of respect for her. Whenever a fellow teacher suggested a new and sound way of dealing with social stud-
ies or science, Grace's answer was always, "Yes--what have we got to lose!" She pulled her share of the load
coming in some days with bandaged leg and all. When kidded by the other teachers she always had a quick and
witty retort. Many children when quizzed about Mrs. Taylor will attest to the fact that she enjoyed children, loved
her subject, and inspired a love of knowledge in them.
We all wish her years and years of richly deserved happiness and leisure.

TWENTY-FIVE YEARS AGO (Town Report Flashback - 1947)
Superintendent of Schools: Mr. Charles M. Robinson
Enrollment, January 5, 1948:
School Budget for 1948: $77,350. Estimated Receipts - $18,326.27
Grades 1-6 - 298
Number of Teachers (including Principals) employed for the 1947-48
Grades 7-12- 218
school year was 21

There were 25 graduates in the Class of 1947
Excerpts from Report of Superintendent of Schools:
"We believe the Town of Lunenburg is fortunate in having such a good teaching staff and urge the citizens to
cooperate with them in their many difficult problems We have been very fortunate during the past year in having
student instructors from the Fitchburg State Teachers' College to handle our shop classes. Excellent work has been
accomplished, real enthusiasm shown by the students, and shop facilities have been improved under the guidance of
these men. In March and April, 1947, the Boston University Cooperative Guidance and Testing Service adminis -
tered two sets of tests to the pupils in the Jr.-Sr. High. The first series, for grades 8 and 9, tested mental ability,
reading comprehension, English and Math fundamentals. The second, for grade 11, was for occupational interest and
aptitude, but included also mental ability and reading comprehension Early in the year alterations were made at
the Brooks House to accoramodate Grade, 6 in that building, Grade 3 was moved to the second floor in the Center
School, and a combined first and second grade class was formed.

===PAGE 118===
School Nursing Report
for 1972
E.NICBEL
The "Lunenburg Public Health Nursing Service" has two full-time Registered Nurses-Mrs. Doris Lahti and
Mrs. Gladys Barney, and one part-time Registered Nurse--Mrs. Shirley Carpenter, who works five hours a day
when schools are in session.
The above staff is hired by the Town to perform Visiting Nursing as well as School Nursing.
The Nashoba Associated Boards of Health offer the following programs in the school program--which require
signed permission from a parent with the exception of the Educational Dental Programs:
German Measles (Rubella) and Mumps Vaccine to certain grades
Grade 1 only - Trivalent Oral Polio Series or Polio Booster for those who have previously
completed oral series
Grades 2, 5, 8 - Dental Fluoride Program given by Dental Hygienist
Grades 1 & 4 - Educational Dental Programs by Dental Hygienist
Grade 1- Tine Testing
Grades 1 & 11- Diphtheria and Tetanus Booster and Small Pox re-vaccination
We are fortunate in being able to offer these programs to our students which keeps Lunenburg Schools immuni -
zation rate at a high level.
Dr. John DeLeo, School Physician, annually examines students in Grades 4, 7, and 10, with the exception
of those students who have returned a Physical Form completed and signed by their own doctor. Dr. DeLeo also
examines all athletes, who also have blood pressure taken and urine "tes-taped" for sugar.
Vision and Hearing Testing is done annually on all students, Kindergarten through Grade 12. Notices of
failures are mailed home to parents, with a doctor's form attached. This form must be returned to the Nurses' Office
after the child has been seen by an Eye or Ear doctor in order for the school to follow the doctor's recommendations.
In 1972, "The Future Nurses Club" at the High School met twice a month during activity period with their ad-
visor, Mrs. Gladys Barney. In an attempt to create interest in medical fields, speakers and films were used, A major
project undertaken by these girls was the making and delivering of May baskets for the Pearl Brook Housing Program.
Without the help of the Administration and School Personnel, the School Health Program could not be carried
out successfully. We thank you all.
Respectfully submitted,
DORIS M. LAHTI, R. N.
Supervising Nurse

Excerpts from Report of Superintendent of Schools:
"We live in a time when probably the greatest asset remaining to America is the potential which lies inits
youth. Without question, our future greatness, yes, even our survival as a great nation depends on them. On US lies
the burdens and responsibility to think and see big enough that Lunenburg children receive the advantages of educa-
tion which are second to none--So often the efforts and energies of the School Committee and the Superintendent of
Schools can be bogged down in the business of the so-called three "B's" of modern day schools, namely: "Babies,
Budgets and Buses", It always seems that the School Committee is confronted with increased enrollments, therefore,
larger Budgets and more Transportation
Additional school housing at the Elementary level will be required within
the next two to three years The School Facilities Committee is constantly evaluating this problem One excep-
tionally bright spot this year has come about with the remodeling of the Central Administrative Offices at the Brooks
House. Any citizen who has had the opportunity to examine these facilities, will, I am sure, be more than pleased
with the results. =

===PAGE 122===
School Lunch Program Report
for 1972
The 1971-72 school year has been a successful and changing one for
the School Lunch Program.
In the period of 167 lunch days, we served 226,029 Type "A"
lunches to students and 11,088 Type "A" lunches to adults. We purchased
277,601 one-half pints of milk which includes the special milk sales.
We now employ twenty-three full-time cafeteria workers with a
cook-manager and a baker, along with general workers in each school.
We purchased very little. bread last year as our bakers do a great job on dinner rolls, hamburg and hot dog
rolls, and french bread, as well as cakes, cookies and other fine desserts.
Early in the 1971-72 school year we started planning for the Satellite Hot-Lunch Program for the Annex. We
experimented during the Thanksgiving and Christmas lunches as well as a few other times. This program, to the de-
light of most of the third graders, started on a daily basis on April 24, 1972. The lunches are cooked in the Turkey
Hill Middle School kitchen and then are packaged individually and put into insulated metal containers in order to
keep them hot or cold. Two ladies from the cafeteria travel to the Annex with the lunches and serve them to the
children.
In another development, rising food and labor costs as well as fewer government-supplied commodities, made
it difficult for the program to meet its financial obligations and therefore, permission was requested from the State
Bureau of Food Services to raise the price of lunches from 30¢ to 35¢ for students and 50¢ to 55¢ for adults. With
the granting of this permission, our new prices went into effect on April 3, 1972. The 5¢ raise was a big factor in
keeping our program on a self-paying basis.
The cook-managers from the schools were given the
opportunity the last week of June to attend the Summer Con -
ference at the University of Massachusetts sponsored by the
State School Lunch Office. Mrs. Nass from the Elementary
School and Mrs. Haueisen from the Middle School took advan-
fage of this worthwhile opportunity.
In closing, I wish to thank Dr. Holland, our Superinten-
dent, Mr. Goodrich, Administrative Assistant, Mrs. Ginny
Welch and office staff, Town Personnel, Custodians, and
School Lunch Personnel, for their help in keeping our program
a success.
E.N.
Respectfully submitted,
(Mrs.) LUCY W. CHARTRAND
School Lunch Supervisor
